The task: prove strong normalization of STLC CBV. Locally nameless with fvars, typing context is atom -> option ty (no binder for bvar! interesting — typing only handles fvar and open terms). Note: `has_type` has no rule for `tm_bvar`. So typed terms are locally closed in a sense.

Plan: We need to prove `forall t T, empty |-- t \in T -> SN t`.

Standard approach: logical relation over closed terms? But the typing relation is about fvars, and terms may contain free variables (fvars) if context non-empty. For empty context, terms could still contain fvars? Typing `empty |-- t \in T` with T_Var requires Gamma x = Some T, and empty x = None, so no free fvar can be typed. But under T_Abs, the body gets fvar x with a non-empty context, so the fundamental theorem must handle arbitrary contexts with the variable interpretation.

Also, terms in the relation: Since step requires locally_closed for app argument and abs... Note `ST_AppAbs` requires locally_closed (tm_abs T t) and value v. `ST_App1` requires locally_closed t2. So terms with free fvars can still step, actually fvars are locally closed (lc_fvar). So locally_closed doesn't mean closed w.r.t. fvars. Good — fvars are like constants. So the whole development can treat fvars as atoms/constants; substitution of fvars via `open`? Wait, the semantics uses `open t v` where open replaces bvar 0 by v. The typing uses `open t1 (tm_fvar x)` for the body. So the semantics substitutes an arbitrary value v (which may contain fvars) for bvar 0, while typing substitutes fvar x. Standard locally nameless.

Since terms under consideration for SN are those typed in `empty`. But the logical relation needs to interpret contexts with fvars and consider substitutions for fvars? Hmm. Actually there's no fvar-substitution operation in the language. The typing environment maps atoms to types; the variable interpretation will be a relation on terms substituted for fvar x. But `open` substitutes bvars only.

Standard approach for locally nameless STLC SN: Define the logical relation on closed (bv-closed? or fully closed?) terms. Options:

1. Define LR on terms with free variables allowed, treating fvars as opaque and context interpretation via assignments of types to fvars. But fvar x needs to be in the relation — that requires the LR to be defined for open terms, i.e., we need to relate fvars. A common trick: define LR over terms with a closing substitution for fvars? Actually the typical locally-nameless SN proof (e.g., in Software Foundations "Stlc" with locally nameless, strong normalization chapter?) uses:

- `Definition normalizing` etc.
- Logical relation `R T t` defined only for locally closed terms? But fvars are locally closed. For a variable, `R T (fvar x)` cannot be established without knowing the context.

The usual approach: define the relation on "closing substitutions" of fvars: A term t is in relation at T under context Gamma if for all substitutions sigma mapping fvars to values/terms that are in the relation at their types (Gamma x), ... but fvar substitution isn't defined in this language. Approach A: Use standard Girard-Tait logical relation with an explicit fvar substitution.

Define:
- `subst_fvar (sigma : atom -> tm) : tm -> tm` replacing fvar x with sigma x, recursively (no capture issues since fvars are free and substitution of fvars doesn't capture bvars; it's just a homomorphism).
- Value substitution via `open`.

Define LR:
```
Fixpoint LR (T : ty) : tm -> Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Bool => fun t => SN t /\ (t = tm_true \/ t = tm_false \/ exists t', ... )? 
```

Standard Tait for CBV: define SN directly for ground type: `R_Bool := SN` restricted to terms of type Bool but since every term at Bool is SN iff... Standard definition for CBV STLC SN (e.g., in Pierce's TAPL chapter on normalization? or the "Normalization" chapter):
```
R_Bool(t) = SN(t)      (or t normalizes to true/false)
R_{A->B}(t) = SN(t) /\ forall u, R_A(u) -> R_B(t u)
```

So the fundamental theorem statement:

```
Theorem fundamental : forall Gamma t T,
  has_type Gamma t T ->
  forall sigma, (forall x U, Gamma x = Some U -> R U (sigma x)) ->
  R T (subst_fvar sigma t).
```

So the core is the "application of a value at arrow relation" lemma. Let me think through it carefully with CBV.

Then goal: R B (app (abs A t1s) u0) for R A u0.

```
Lemma R_app_abs : forall A B (t u : tm),
  locally_closed (tm_abs A t) ->
  (forall v, locally_closed v -> R A v -> R B (open t v)) ->
  locally_closed u ->
  R A u ->
  R B (tm_app (tm_abs A t) u).

  intros A B t u Hlc Hbody Hlcu HAu.

  revert Hlcu HAu.
  induction HSN as [u Hsteps IH]; intros Hlcu HAu.
  ...
```

Hmm, but actually there's an even cleaner way to state the commutation: subst_sigma (open t (fvar x)) = open (subst_sigma t) (sigma x) when? Let's compute: LHS substitutes fvars in open t (fvar x). open t (fvar x) = open_rec 0 (fvar x) t; substituting fvars: subst commutes with open_rec: subst sigma (open_rec k u t) = open_rec k (subst sigma u) (subst sigma t). Let me step back and think about a cleaner logical relation for CBV SN that is well-known to work.

Maybe use a different relation: define R via "all applications of the term to R-arguments are SN" instead of "the term applied once is R". Specifically:

For CBV, one common definition: define a set of terms that are "strongly normalizing and, at arrow types, map related values to related terms". Alternatively use *values* in the LR:

Standard Girard-Tait for CBV (as in, e.g., "A proof of strong normalisation" exercises):
- `SN` at base.
- `R(A->B) := { t | SN t and for all v ∈ R(A) with v a value? , t v ∈ R(B) }`.
But the same issues.

Hmm, maybe there's a much simpler observation for this specific language: strong normalization of CBV STLC can be proven by a *syntactic* translation/measure: because there's no recursion and no if, every term's reduction is finite. Could we define a function that computes the "maximum reduction length" by structural recursion on typed terms using the substitution structure? For STLC SN, one can define a measure: for a term t, define `sz t` = size, and show each step decreases some lexicographic measure? For beta, size decreases. For CBV, app steps reduce subterms; but beta: size(app(abs t) v) = 1 + size(abs t) + size(v) → size(open t v) which can be larger than size(t) because substituting v multiple times duplicates it! Classic: |open t v| can exceed |app(abs t) v| if t uses bvar 0 multiple times. So a simple size measure doesn't work; need a type-based measure (like the standard "depth of types" / Girard's).

Alternative: prove SN via a "hereditarily size-bounded" relation, i.e., the logical relation again. We need to get the technical details right.

Let me reconsider. Maybe define R so that the arrow case says: t ∈ R(A->B) iff SN t and for all u ∈ R(A), t u ∈ R(B), and prove the app-abs lemma by a *double induction*: on the type T2 and the SN derivation of u. There might be a way to handle the nested application without backward closure by directly analyzing the SN of nested applications.

Specifically, the problematic goal: R B (app s w) where s = app (abs T1 t) u, in the case ~value u. We want to prove it via SN-induction on u, but note that the term app s w has "u" inside; the CBV reduction of app s w: since s = app(abs,u) and s is not a value (app never value), the outer app can only reduce via ST_App1, requiring s --> s'. s's reducts: if u value → open t u; if u --> u' → app(abs, u'). So reductions of app s w are governed by u's reductions. This suggests an induction on SN u with a *more general statement*: for all contexts C (applications), R ... Hmm.

Alternatively, note that app s w where s = app(abs t) u: in CBV, this is stuck until u reduces to a value; then it beta-reduces. So the term's reduction is like: reduce u to a value, then app(abs,u_val) w... Actually the outer app's function is s; to apply s to w, first s must reduce to a value. s = app(abs t) u: CBV evaluates the function position first? ST_App1 reduces t1 = app(abs,u) while t2 = w is locally closed. So s reduces by evaluating u (since abs is value), until u is a value, then s --> open t u, which is not necessarily a value; then app s w continues reducing with function s' = open t u. Hmm, complicated.

Maybe there's a smarter definition where the arrow case is just SN plus a *value-based* condition? Since CBV only substitutes values, perhaps define R only on values at arrow types, and separately require SN for terms:

Standard approach in TAPL for CBV normalization? Let me recall the actual literature: For call-by-value STLC, one can prove SN via a logical relation where:
- `R_Bool = { t | t SN }` (closed terms)
- `R_{A->B} = { t | t SN and for all v ∈ V_A, t v ∈ R_B }` where V_A = R_A ∩ values.
But substitution of a value v into the body: body open t v; since v is a value, and the typing of the body with fvar x opened... The fundamental theorem would be stated for closed terms (no fvars) or with the fvar trick.

The nested application issue arises because R at arrow is defined via arbitrary R arguments, not just values. If we restrict the arrow relation's app property to *values*, then the problematic app-abs lemma becomes: given v ∈ V_A (value and R A), show app(abs t) v ∈ R B. That's easier: app(abs t) v --> open t v, and open t v ∈ R B from IH/Hbody. Then backward closure? Or just SN + ... Hmm, but the fundamental theorem for T_App needs: t1 ∈ R(A->B), t2 ∈ R(A) where t2 is an arbitrary (not necessarily value) term, and conclude t1 t2 ∈ R(B). With the value-restricted arrow relation, this doesn't follow directly. Standard solution: prove a lemma "if t ∈ R A then t evaluates (in some sense) to a value in R A" or "R A is closed under CBV evaluation and t ∈ R A iff SN t and ...". Hmm.

Alternatively, use the standard "reducibility candidates" style where R is defined for *closed* terms and one proves: if t ∈ R A then SN t, and if t SN and t -->* v with v value then v ∈ R A? For instance, at base R_Bool could be all SN terms, so any SN term of type Bool is in R. At arrow, need t SN and for all value v ∈ V_A, t v ∈ R_B. Then t1 t2 with t1 ∈ R(A->B), t2 ∈ R A: since t2 SN, it has a value... well, not necessarily normalizes to a value (stuck terms). But in the fundamental theorem, terms come from typed terms; a typed term is either a value or steps, and SN implies it normalizes to a value (if it has no fvars/stuck?). So a typed term like `fvar x` with x : Bool is stuck (no step, not a value). So evaluation doesn't reach a value. But such terms can still be in the relation if the relation includes them.

Hmm OK. Let me look at this from a different angle. Since the language is small and CBV is deterministic, maybe define the relation using a *strongly normalizing closure* notion with "neutral" terms, like Girard's reducibility candidates for CBV:

Neutral terms: not values (apps and fvars and bvars). Standard reducibility candidates for CBV (e.g., in the proof of normalization for the λv-calculus): a term is in a candidate if it's SN and, when it's neutral, all its reducts are in the candidate; for values, additional conditions at arrow types (a λ is in R(A->B) if its body applied to any value in V(A) is in R(B)). This is the standard "reducibility candidate" definition, and it's well-founded because for values at arrow type we test application to values.

Let me define:
```
Inductive/Definition R T t :=
  match T with
  | Bool => SN t
  | A -> B => SN t /\ (forall v, value v -> R A v -> R B (app t v))
  end
```
with the idea that for a lambda abs A body, app (abs A body) v --> open body v, and we need R B (open body v) which comes from the fundamental theorem at the body with sigma mapping the opened variable to v.

Then T_App: t1 ∈ R(A->B) gives forall v value, R A v -> R B (t1 v). But t2 arbitrary ∈ R A. If t2 is a value, done directly. If not, then t2 is neutral and SN; its reducts are in R A (by candidate closure). Then t1 t2 reduces: ST_App1 if t1 steps; ST_App2 if t1 value and t2 steps. Hmm, need to show R B(t1 t2) for t1 ∈ R(A->B), t2 ∈ R A.

Standard candidate approach defines candidates as sets of SN terms closed under reduction in the right way, and proves t1 ∈ R(A->B), t2 ∈ R A implies t1 t2 ∈ R B — this is often an explicit closure property called "R is a reducibility candidate and application is compatible". Let's think for our concrete R:

Given t1 ∈ R(A->B): SN t1, and forall v value∈R A, R B(t1 v).
Given t2 ∈ R A: SN t2.
Goal: R B(t1 t2).

If B = Bool: need SN(t1 t2). Since hmm, t1 t2's reductions: either t1 steps (IH on SN t1) or t1 value (abs) and t2 steps (IH on SN t2) or both values → beta: t1 t2 --> open body t2; need R Bool(open body t2) = SN. We know R Bool(open body t2) if t2 ∈ R A and t2 is a value — yes in this case. But wait, we need t2 value for the arrow property, and here t1 = abs and t2 is a value. So R Bool(open body t2) → SN. Then t1 t2 has this reduct SN, but other reducts? CBV deterministic-ish; the SN proof can be by induction on SN t1 and SN t2 with case analysis of step. If t1 not a value: t1 t2 --> t1' t2 with t1 --> t1'; need R B(t1' t2) — by induction on SN t1, if we know t1' ∈ R(A->B) (forward closure) and t2 ∈ R A. If t1 is a value (abs) and t2 not value: t2 --> t2', app --> app t1 t2', need R B(t1 t2'): IH on SN t2 with t2' ∈ R A (forward closure) — but t1 ∈ R(A->B) — need closure under reduction of the second argument? Just apply the IH on t2's SN with the same t1. If both values: beta, arrow property. So the proof is by nested SN induction on t1 and t2. This is the standard "compatibility lemma": R(A->B) · R(A) ⊆ R(B), proven by double induction on SN derivations.

Crucially, if the arrow property is restricted to values v, then in the beta case only value arguments are needed. And the induction works because in the App1 case, t1' ∈ R(A->B) via forward closure, and SN t1' is smaller. In the App2 case, t2' ∈ R A, SN t2' smaller. This is provable!

Since x0 ∉ fv t1, subst sigma'' t1 = subst sigma t1. 
So R T2 (open (subst sigma t1) v) = R T2 (open body_s v). ✓.

Let me now also consider: does R_of_neutral's statement need any restriction to avoid the circularity? Statement:
```
Lemma R_of_neutral : forall T s,
  strongly_normalizing s ->
  ~ value s ->
  (forall s', s --> s' -> R T s') ->
  R T s.
```
Proof by induction on T.
- Bool: R Bool s = SN s ✓.
- Arrow A B: R (A->B) s = SN s /\ forall w, value w -> R A w -> R B (app s w).
  SN s ✓. For w value with R A w:
    Need R B (app s w). Apply IHB to `app s w`:
      - SN (app s w): SN_intro; intros s'' Hstep; case on Hstep:
        + ST_AppAbs: premise s = tm_abs T t with lc, so value s via v_abs — contradicts Hnv.
        + ST_App1 Hs Hlc: s'' = app s' w with s --> s'. Hred s' Hs : R (A->B) s'. Its arrow property gives R B (app s' w) (using Hwv, HAw). R_SN B gives SN. ✓.
        + ST_App2 Hv Hs: Hv : value s — contradicts Hnv.
      - ~ value (app s w): apps not values. Prove lemma `app_not_value : forall t1 t2, ~ value (tm_app t1 t2)` by intro; inversion.
      - forall s'', app s w --> s'' -> R B s'': case:
        + ST_AppAbs: value s contradiction.
        + ST_App1 Hs: s'' = app s' w; R B from Hred s' arrow property. ✓.
        + ST_App2: value s contradiction.
    Then IHB gives R B (app s w). ✓.

Wait: I used `IHB` (induction hypothesis for B) — but the lemma is by induction on T = A->B, so IHB is the lemma at B. Note the lemma includes the forall s' step -> R B s' premise; for app s w we supply it. ✓.

Important: in the ST_App1 case, Hred s' Hs : R (A->B) s'. To get R B(app s' w), we use R (A->B) s' = SN s' /\ forall w, value w -> R A w -> R B(app s' w). We have Hwv : value w and HAw : R A w. ✓.

Good. Note that the lemma's induction is on the type T, and the recursive call at B is on a smaller type. ✓. But wait: the lemma at B has hypothesis SN(app s w) which we proved; ~value; all steps. ✓.

Now R_abs_app:

Proof: Have SN u from R_SN. Do SN induction on u, generalizing? Goal P u := R T1 u -> R T2 (app abs u). Use the SN_ind principle:
```
revert Hu? 
```
Let me write:

```
assert (HSN : SN u) by apply R_SN; auto.

```
Hmm, but `induction HSN` with HSN : SN u and goal `R T1 u -> R T2 (app ... u)`: the induction principle SN_ind will abstract u in the goal. Since the goal has u in it, fine.

Then: want R T2(app abs u). Case analysis: value u?
- value u: Apply R_of_neutral T2 (app abs u):
   - SN(app abs u): SN_intro; intros s' Hstep; case Hstep:
     * ST_AppAbs Hlc Hv: s' = open body u. Hbody u Hu : R T2(open body u). R_SN. ✓

   - ~value(app abs u) ✓.
   - forall s', app abs u --> s' -> R T2 s': case Hstep:
     * ST_AppAbs → Hbody u Hu.
     * ST_App1 → abs_no_step.
     * ST_App2 → value_no_step.
   Then R_of_neutral gives R T2(app abs u). ✓.
- ~value u: Apply R_of_neutral T2 (app abs u):
   - SN(app abs u): SN_intro; intros s' Hstep; case:
     * ST_AppAbs: requires value u — contradiction.
     * ST_App1: abs_no_step.
     * ST_App2 Hv Hs: s' = app abs u'; need SN s'. IH Hs : R T1 u' -> R T2(app abs u') (since IH : forall u', u --> u' -> P u'). Hu' : R T1 u' from R_step_fwd T1 Hu Hs. So R T2(app abs u') → R_SN → SN. ✓.
   - ~value(app abs u) ✓.
   - forall s', step -> R T2 s': same cases; App2: IH Hs Hu' : R T2(app abs u') ✓.
   Then R_of_neutral. ✓.

Great. But note the SN_ind IH gives P u' for reducts u' with u --> u'. But is `induction HSN` going to give IH in the form forall u', step u u' -> P u'? The SN induction principle is:

```
SN_ind : forall P : tm -> Prop,
  (forall t, (forall t', step t t' -> SN t') -> (forall t', step t t' -> P t') -> P t) ->
  forall t, SN t -> P t
```
We can use `induction HSN as [u Hsteps IH]`? The constructor SN_intro has two arguments: the term t and the function `forall t', t --> t' -> SN t'`. When doing induction, Coq will give hypotheses for each recursive occurrence? Hmm, the induction principle generated for SN is:

```
SN_ind : forall P, (forall t, (forall t', step t t' -> SN t') -> (forall t', step t t' -> P t') -> P t) -> forall t, SN t -> P t
```
Actually, when a constructor has a non-uniform recursive occurrence under a forall, Coq generates an induction principle with `(forall t', R t t' -> P t')` as a hypothesis. Let me recall: `Inductive SN : tm -> Prop := SN_intro : forall t, (forall t', step t t' -> SN t') -> SN t.` The generated `SN_ind`:
```
SN_ind : forall P : tm -> Prop,
  (forall t : tm,
      (forall t' : tm, step t t' -> SN t') ->
      (forall t' : tm, step t t' -> P t') ->
      P t) ->
  forall t : tm, SN t -> P t
```
Yes, because the recursive argument occurs in a forall-premise (strictly positive), Coq generates the IH as `forall t', step t t' -> P t'`. So `induction HSN as [u Hsteps IH]` gives Hsteps : forall t', step u t' -> SN t' and IH : forall t', step u t' -> P t'. 

Goal after `revert Hu`? Let's set P u := R T1 u -> R T2 (app (abs T1 body) u). Then `induction HSN as [u Hsteps IH]` should leave goal P u, i.e., R T1 u -> R T2(...). Then `intros Hu`. And IH : forall u', u --> u' -> P u'.

Let me define fv:
```
Fixpoint fv (t : tm) : list atom :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar _ => nil
  | tm_fvar x => cons x nil
  | tm_app t1 t2 => app (fv t1) (fv t2)
  | tm_abs _ t1 => fv t1
  | tm_true | tm_false => nil
  end.
```
Lemmas:
- `fv_subst : forall sigma t, fv (subst_fvar sigma t) = flat_map (fun x => fv (sigma x)) (fv t)`? Might be useful but maybe not needed.
- `subst_fvar_agree : forall sigma1 sigma2 t, (forall x, In x (fv t) -> sigma1 x = sigma2 x) -> subst_fvar sigma1 t = subst_fvar sigma2 t`. Needed to show subst sigma'' t1 = subst sigma t1 when x0 ∉ fv t1. Prove by induction on t1. Straightforward.

Also `fv_open`? Not needed.

- `not_in_fv_fvar` etc.

Now the substitution function:
```
Fixpoint subst_fvar (sigma : atom -> tm) (t : tm) : tm :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar i => tm_bvar i
  | tm_fvar x => sigma x
  | tm_app t1 t2 => tm_app (subst_fvar sigma t1) (subst_fvar sigma t2)
  | tm_abs T t1 => tm_abs T (subst_fvar sigma t1)
  | tm_true => tm_true
  | tm_false => tm_false
  end.
```
Wait: does fvar substitution under a binder need care? The body's bvars are unchanged, and fvars are global; substituting fvar x by sigma x is fine without shifting. ✓.

Also, we need to think about whether `R` should be defined via a Fixpoint on ty with a Prop result. Coq accepts:
```
Fixpoint R (T : ty) (t : tm) : Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Bool => strongly_normalizing t
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 => strongly_normalizing t /\ forall v, value v -> R T1 v -> R T2 (tm_app t v)
  end.
```
The recursive calls R T1 and R T2 are on subterms of T — structurally decreasing ✓. Note the `forall v, value v -> R T1 v -> R T2 (tm_app t v)` — R T1 appears in a negative-ish position (premise), but this is a Fixpoint returning Prop, not an Inductive, so no positivity requirement. Coq allows it. ✓.

Potential issue: R T1 appears as an argument to the fixpoint in a premise; termination check only cares about the type argument T decreasing. ✓.

Now, the induction on has_type: the fundamental theorem:
```
Theorem fundamental : forall Gamma t T,
  has_type Gamma t T ->
  forall sigma,
    (forall x U, Gamma x = Some U -> R U (sigma x)) ->
    R T (subst_fvar sigma t).
Proof.
  intros Gamma t T H.
  induction H as [...]; intros sigma Hgood.
```
Wait, the induction principle: has_type is an inductive with constructors indexed. `induction H` will abstract the indices and give IHs for the recursive premises. Let's carefully handle the T_Abs constructor which has a dependent premise `forall x, ~ In x L -> has_type (update Gamma x T1) (open t1 (fvar x)) T2`. The induction hypothesis will be:

IH : forall x, ~ In x L -> forall sigma, good (update Gamma x T1) sigma -> R T2 (subst sigma (open t1 (fvar x)))

Actually the IH generated by induction on H for a premise that is a forall over x of a typing... Since has_type is in Prop and the premise is itself a forall, the induction principle of has_type gives:

T_Abs case: 
```
forall (L : list atom) (Gamma : context) (T1 T2 : ty) (t1 : tm),
  (forall x : atom, ~ In x L -> has_type (update Gamma x T1) (open t1 (fvar x)) T2) ->
  (forall x : atom, ~ In x L -> forall sigma, good (update Gamma x T1) sigma -> R T2 (subst_fvar sigma (open t1 (fvar x)))) ->
  forall sigma, good Gamma sigma -> R (T1 -> T2) (subst_fvar sigma (tm_abs T1 t1))
```
Yes, Coq's induction on an inductive family with a premise that's a forall of a recursive occurrence under a binder: I believe it will produce IH with the forall and the recursive occurrence replaced by the motive. The motive is P Gamma t T := forall sigma, good Gamma sigma -> R T (subst sigma t). The recursive occurrence `has_type (update Gamma x T1) (open t1 (fvar x)) T2` appears under `forall x, ~In x L ->`. Coq's induction principle should give the IH as forall x, ~In x L -> P (update Gamma x T1) (open t1 (fvar x)) T2 = forall x, ~In x L -> forall sigma, good ... -> R T2 (subst sigma (open t1 (fvar x))). 

Hmm, but there's a subtlety: Coq's `induction H` when indices include Gamma (a function) and the motive involves them... It should be fine. But sometimes Coq fails to generalize the right things and gives "cannot find the elimination scheme" or produces IHs with the un-generalized sigma. Let's be prepared to use `induction H` with explicit motive via `induction H as [...]`. Since the goal after `intros ... H` is `forall sigma, good Gamma sigma -> R T (subst sigma t)`, and `induction H` will abstract Gamma, t, T in the goal — the goal has `subst_fvar sigma t` and `Gamma`, and `T`; these are all indices/params. Coq should generalize. But `sigma` and `Hgood` are in the context; we must revert them first:
```
intros Gamma t T H. induction H; intros sigma Hgood.
```
Actually `induction H` before intros sigma: the goal is `forall sigma, good Gamma sigma -> ...`; Coq's induction will generalize the goal over the indices appearing in it, but the `forall sigma` is part of the goal — it might remain and be reintroduced. Typically you do:
```
intros Gamma t T H.
induction H; intros sigma Hgood.
```
This should work: `induction H` will clear/revert sigma? There's no sigma in context yet. The goal is a forall over sigma, so the motive is `fun Gamma t T => forall sigma, ...`. Then each case gets the forall goal. Fine.

Potential issue: the T_Abs case's IH must be usable at the specific x0 with ~In x0 L. ✓.

Let me now also consider whether the fundamental theorem's `subst_fvar sigma t` for t under binders is correct. In T_Abs, the body t1 is substituted with sigma directly (no shift), because fvar substitution is capture-free w.r.t. bvars. ✓. The commutation lemma uses subst_fvar sigma (open t1 (fvar x)) = open (subst sigma t1) (subst sigma (fvar x)) — note open t1 (fvar x) = open_rec 0 (fvar x) t1. The lemma's RHS: open (subst sigma t1) (subst sigma (fvar x)) = open_rec 0 (sigma x) (subst sigma t1). ✓.

Let me double-check the crucial commutation: subst_fvar sigma (open_rec k u t) = open_rec k (subst_fvar sigma u) (subst_fvar sigma t). In the bvar case: open_rec k u (bvar i) = if eqb k i then u else bvar i. subst of that = if eqb k i then subst u else bvar i. RHS: open_rec k (subst u) (bvar i) = if eqb k i then subst u else bvar i. ✓. Need to destruct (Nat.eqb k i) in the proof. In the abs case: open_rec k u (abs T t) = abs T (open_rec (S k) u t); subst = abs T (subst (open_rec (S k) u t)); IH with S k gives abs T (open_rec (S k) (subst u) (subst t)); RHS = open_rec k (subst u) (abs T (subst t)) = abs T (open_rec (S k) (subst u) (subst t)). ✓. So the proof needs the IH at (S k) — if we do induction on t with k universally quantified (i.e., `induction t; intros k u`), then the IH is forall k u. But Coq's `induction t` before intros gives IHt : forall k u, ... ✓.

```coq
(** STLC call-by-value strong-normalization benchmark. ... *)

From Stdlib Require Import Arith.PeanoNat.
From Stdlib Require Import Lists.List.
From Stdlib Require Import Relations.Relation_Definitions.

Module STLCNormalizationNoneHardTask.

... (unchanged) ...

Inductive strongly_normalizing : tm -> Prop :=
  | SN_intro : forall t,
      (forall t', t --> t' -> strongly_normalizing t') ->
      strongly_normalizing t.

(* ===== Solution additions ===== *)

(** Fvar substitution. *)
Fixpoint subst_fvar (sigma : atom -> tm) (t : tm) : tm :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar i => tm_bvar i
  | tm_fvar x => sigma x
  | tm_app t1 t2 => tm_app (subst_fvar sigma t1) (subst_fvar sigma t2)
  | tm_abs T t1 => tm_abs T (subst_fvar sigma t1)
  | tm_true => tm_true
  | tm_false => tm_false
  end.

(** Free atoms of a term. *)
Fixpoint fv (t : tm) : list atom :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar _ => nil
  | tm_fvar x => x :: nil
  | tm_app t1 t2 => fv t1 ++ fv t2
  | tm_abs _ t1 => fv t1
  | tm_true => nil
  | tm_false => nil
  end.

Lemma subst_fvar_open_rec : forall sigma k t u,
  subst_fvar sigma (open_rec k u t) =
  open_rec k (subst_fvar sigma u) (subst_fvar sigma t).

  - reflexivity.
```

Let me think about `fundamental`'s statement carefully, including the `good` predicate:

```
Definition good (Gamma : context) (sigma : atom -> tm) : Prop :=
  forall x U, Gamma x = Some U -> R U (sigma x).
```

Then:
```
Lemma fundamental : forall Gamma t T,
  has_type Gamma t T ->
  forall sigma, good Gamma sigma -> R T (subst_fvar sigma t).
```

Proof by induction on H. Let's write out each case with tactics and be ready to adjust.

Case T_Var:

The number of arguments for each constructor:

The recursive occurrence is inside the forall-premise, so the induction scheme's case will include an IH after the premise: likely args are L Gamma T1 T2 t1 Hbody IHbody? Hmm, how does Coq order the IH? In the standard induction principle, each recursive argument gets an IH immediately after it. Here the recursive argument is the whole premise `forall x, ~ In x L -> has_type ...`, which is a function whose result is a recursive occurrence. Coq's generated induction principle for such nested occurrences: it creates the IH as another argument after the premise, with the IH being `forall x, ~ In x L -> P (update Gamma x T1) (open t1 (fvar x)) T2`. When using `induction H as [...]`, pattern syntax: for T_Abs, the pattern would be `L Gamma T1 T2 t1 Hbody IHbody`. I believe the IH is placed right after the hypothesis. Let's test.

Similarly T_App: forall Gamma t1 t2 T1 T2, has_type ... -> has_type ... -> has_type ...; pattern: Gamma t1 t2 T1 T2 H1 IH1 H2 IH2.

Let me draft the entire added section carefully.

```coq
(** ** Auxiliary definitions and lemmas for strong normalization. *)

(** Substitution of free (named) variables.  Because locally nameless
    binders use [tm_bvar], substitution for free variables is a plain
    homomorphism and never needs shifting. *)

Fixpoint subst_fvar (sigma : atom -> tm) (t : tm) : tm :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar i => tm_bvar i
  | tm_fvar x => sigma x
  | tm_app t1 t2 => tm_app (subst_fvar sigma t1) (subst_fvar sigma t2)
  | tm_abs T t1 => tm_abs T (subst_fvar sigma t1)
  | tm_true => tm_true
  | tm_false => tm_false
  end.

(** Free atoms of a term. *)

Fixpoint fv (t : tm) : list atom :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar _ => nil
  | tm_fvar x => x :: nil
  | tm_app t1 t2 => fv t1 ++ fv t2
  | tm_abs _ t1 => fv t1
  | tm_true => nil
  | tm_false => nil
  end.

(** A fresh atom for a finite list of atoms. *)

Fixpoint list_max (L : list atom) : atom :=
  match L with
  | nil => 0
  | x :: L' => Nat.max x (list_max L')
  end.

Definition fresh (L : list atom) : atom := S (list_max L).

Lemma in_list_max : forall L x, In x L -> x <= list_max L.
Proof.
  induction L as [| y L IH]; intros x Hx; simpl in *.
  - contradiction.
  - destruct Hx as [-> | Hx].
    + apply Nat.le_max_l.
    + apply Nat.le_trans with (list_max L); [apply IH; exact Hx | apply Nat.le_max_r].
Qed.

Lemma fresh_not_in : forall L, ~ In (fresh L) L.
Proof.
  intros L Hin. apply in_list_max in Hin. unfold fresh in Hin. lia.
Qed.
```

```
  subst_fvar sigma (open_rec k u t) =

  - reflexivity.

Qed.
```

```
Lemma subst_fvar_open : forall sigma t u,
  subst_fvar sigma (open t u) = open (subst_fvar sigma t) (subst_fvar sigma u).

Proof.
  induction t as [i|x|t1 IH1 t2 IH2|T t IH| |]; simpl.
  - reflexivity.
  - reflexivity.
  - rewrite IH1, IH2; reflexivity.
  - rewrite IH; reflexivity.
  - reflexivity.
  - reflexivity.
Qed.

Lemma subst_fvar_agree : forall sigma1 sigma2 t,
  (forall x, In x (fv t) -> sigma1 x = sigma2 x) ->
  subst_fvar sigma1 t = subst_fvar sigma2 t.
Proof.
  intros sigma1 sigma2 t. induction t as [i|x|t1 IH1 t2 IH2|T t IH| |]; intros Hagree; simpl.
  - reflexivity.
  - apply Hagree. simpl. auto.
  - f_equal.
    + apply IH1. intros x Hx. apply Hagree. apply in_app_iff. left. exact Hx.
    + apply IH2. intros x Hx. apply Hagree. apply in_app_iff. right. exact Hx.
  - f_equal. apply IH. intros x Hx. apply Hagree. exact Hx.
  - reflexivity.
  - reflexivity.
Qed.
```

For the R lemmas, let me write them concretely.

```
Fixpoint R (T : ty) (t : tm) : Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Bool => strongly_normalizing t
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 =>
      strongly_normalizing t /\
      (forall v, value v -> R T1 v -> R T2 (tm_app t v))
  end.

Lemma R_SN : forall T t, R T t -> strongly_normalizing t.
Proof.
  induction T as [| T1 IH1 T2 IH2]; simpl; intros t H.
  - exact H.
  - destruct H as [HSN _]. exact HSN.
Qed.
```

Wait: `induction T as [| T1 IH1 T2 IH2]` — for ty, constructor Ty_Bool (no args) and Ty_Arrow (ty, ty, with IHs). The `as` pattern for the second case: `T1 IH1 T2 IH2`. In Coq, the induction pattern gives arguments and IHs interleaved: for a constructor with two recursive args, the pattern is `T1 IH1 T2 IH2`. ✓.

SN_step:
```
Lemma SN_step : forall t t', strongly_normalizing t -> t --> t' -> strongly_normalizing t'.
Proof.
  intros t t' HSN Hstep. inversion HSN as [u Hred]. apply Hred. exact Hstep.
Qed.
```
Test needed.

R_step_fwd:
```
Lemma R_step_fwd : forall T t t', R T t -> t --> t' -> R T t'.
Proof.
  induction T as [| T1 IH1 T2 IH2]; simpl; intros t t' HR Hstep.
  - apply SN_step with (t := t); assumption.
  - destruct HR as [HSN Happ].
    split.
    + apply SN_step with (t := t); assumption.
    + intros v Hv HRv.
      apply IH2 with (t := tm_app t v).
      * apply Happ; assumption.
      * apply ST_App1; [exact Hstep | apply value_lc; exact Hv].
```

```
Fixpoint R (T : ty) (t : tm) : Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_Bool => strongly_normalizing t
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 =>
      strongly_normalizing t /\
      (forall v, value v -> R T1 v -> R T2 (tm_app t v))
  end.

Lemma R_SN : forall T t, R T t -> strongly_normalizing t.
Proof.
  induction T as [| T1 IH1 T2 IH2]; simpl; intros t H.
  - exact H.
  - destruct H as [HSN _]. exact HSN.
Qed.
```

```
Lemma step_app_inv : forall t1 t2 t',
  tm_app t1 t2 --> t' ->
  (exists T body v, t1 = tm_abs T body /\ t2 = v /\ t' = open body v /\ locally_closed (tm_abs T body) /\ value v)

  \/ (exists t2', value t1 /\ t2 --> t2' /\ t' = tm_app t1 t2').
Proof.
  intros t1 t2 t' H. inversion H as [T body v Hlc Hv | a a' b Hs Hlcb | v a a' Hv Hs]; subst.
  - left. exists T, body, v. auto.
  - right; left. exists a'. auto.
  - right; right. exists a'. auto.
Qed.
```

```
Lemma step_app_inv : forall s w s',
  tm_app s w --> s' ->
  (exists T body v, s = tm_abs T body /\ w = v /\ s' = open body v /\ locally_closed (tm_abs T body) /\ value v) \/
  (exists s1', s --> s1' /\ s' = tm_app s1' w) \/
  (exists w', value s /\ w --> w' /\ s' = tm_app s w').
```

For using it in R_of_neutral, I think a case-eliminator lemma is cleanest:

```
Lemma step_app_cases : forall t1 t2 t' (P : Prop),
  tm_app t1 t2 --> t' ->
  (forall T body v, t1 = tm_abs T body -> t2 = v -> t' = open body v ->
     locally_closed (tm_abs T body) -> value v -> P) ->
  (forall t1', t1 --> t1' -> t' = tm_app t1' t2 -> locally_closed t2 -> P) ->
  (forall t2', value t1 -> t2 --> t2' -> t' = tm_app t1 t2' -> P) ->
  P.
```
Then in R_of_neutral:
```
apply (step_app_cases s w s' (SN s')).
- intros T0 body v Hs Hw Hs' Hlc Hv. exfalso. apply Hnv. rewrite Hs. apply v_abs. exact Hlc.

```

```

- exact Hstep.  (* step *)

```

```
Lemma R_of_neutral : forall T s,
  strongly_normalizing s ->
  ~ value s ->
  (forall s', s --> s' -> R T s') ->
  R T s.
Proof.
  induction T as [| T1 IH1 T2 IH2]; simpl; intros s HSN Hnv Hred.
  - exact HSN.
  - split.
    + exact HSN.
    + intros w Hwv HRw.
      apply IH2.
      * apply SN_intro. intros s' Hstep.
        refine (step_app_cases s w s' (strongly_normalizing s') Hstep _ _ _).
        -- intros T0 body v Hs Hw Hs' Hlc Hv. subst. exfalso. apply Hnv. apply v_abs. exact Hlc.
        -- intros s1' Hs1 Hs' Hlcw. subst s'.
           apply R_SN with (T := T2).
           destruct (Hred s1' Hs1) as [HSN1 Happ1]. apply Happ1; assumption.
        -- intros w' Hsv Hw' Hs'. subst. exfalso. apply Hnv. exact Hsv.
      * apply app_not_value.
      * intros s' Hstep.
        refine (step_app_cases s w s' (R T2 s') Hstep _ _ _).
        -- intros T0 body v Hs Hw Hs' Hlc Hv. subst. exfalso. apply Hnv. apply v_abs. exact Hlc.
        -- intros s1' Hs1 Hs' Hlcw. subst s'.
           destruct (Hred s1' Hs1) as [HSN1 Happ1]. apply Happ1; assumption.
        -- intros w' Hsv Hw' Hs'. subst. exfalso. apply Hnv. exact Hsv.
Qed.
```

```
Lemma R_app_value : forall A B v t2,
  value v ->
  R (Ty_Arrow A B) v ->
  R A t2 ->
  R B (tm_app v t2).
Proof.
  intros A B v t2 Hv HRv Ht2.
  assert (HSN : strongly_normalizing t2) by (apply R_SN with (T := A); exact Ht2).
  revert Ht2. induction HSN as [t2 Hsteps IH]; intros Ht2.
  destruct (value_dec t2) as [Hv2 | Hnv2].
  - (* t2 is a value: use the arrow property *)
    destruct HRv as [HSNv Happ]. apply Happ; assumption.
  - apply R_of_neutral with (s := tm_app v t2).
    + apply SN_intro. intros s' Hstep.
      refine (step_app_cases v t2 s' (strongly_normalizing s') Hstep _ _ _).
      * intros T0 body v0 Hs Hw Hs' Hlc Hvv. subst v. exfalso. apply Hnv2. rewrite Hw. exact Hvv.
        (* careful: Hs : v = tm_abs T0 body; subst v; Hw : t2 = v0; goal SN s' but we prove contradiction value t2 *)
      * intros s1' Hs1 Hs' Hlcw. exfalso. apply (value_no_step v Hv s1'). exact Hs1.
      * intros t2' Hval Ht2' Hs'. subst s'.
        apply R_SN with (T := B). apply IH; [exact Ht2' | apply R_step_fwd with (t := t2); assumption].
    + apply app_not_value.
    + intros s' Hstep.
      refine (step_app_cases v t2 s' (R B s') Hstep _ _ _).
      * intros T0 body v0 Hs Hw Hs' Hlc Hvv. subst v. exfalso. apply Hnv2. rewrite Hw. exact Hvv.

Qed.
```

```
Lemma R_app : forall A B t1 t2,
  R (Ty_Arrow A B) t1 ->
  R A t2 ->
  R B (tm_app t1 t2).
Proof.
  intros A B t1 t2 H1 H2.
  assert (HSN : strongly_normalizing t1) by (apply R_SN with (T := Ty_Arrow A B); exact H1).
  revert H1 H2. induction HSN as [t1 Hsteps IH]; intros H1 H2.
  destruct (value_dec t1) as [Hv1 | Hnv1].
  - apply R_app_value with (A := A) (B := B); assumption.
  - apply R_of_neutral with (s := tm_app t1 t2).
    + apply SN_intro. intros s' Hstep.
      refine (step_app_cases t1 t2 s' (strongly_normalizing s') Hstep _ _ _).
      * intros T0 body v Hs Hw Hs' Hlc Hvv. subst t1. exfalso. apply Hnv1. apply v_abs. exact Hlc.
      * intros t1' Hs1 Hs' Hlcw. subst s'.

      * intros t2' Hval Ht2' Hs'. subst t1. exfalso. apply Hnv1. exact Hval.

      * intros T0 body v Hs Hw Hs' Hlc Hvv. subst t1. exfalso. apply Hnv1. apply v_abs. exact Hlc.

Qed.
```

Let me now write the fundamental theorem proof concretely for Task.v:

```
Definition good (Gamma : context) (sigma : atom -> tm) : Prop :=
  forall x U, Gamma x = Some U -> R U (sigma x).

Lemma fundamental : forall Gamma t T,
  has_type Gamma t T ->
  forall sigma, good Gamma sigma -> R T (subst_fvar sigma t).
Proof.
  intros Gamma t T H.
  induction H as [Gamma0 x T0 Hlookup | L Gamma0 T1 T2 t1 Hbody IHbody | Gamma0 t1 t2 T1 T2 H1 IH1 H2 IH2 | Gamma0 | Gamma0];
    intros sigma Hgood.
  - (* T_Var *) simpl. apply Hgood. exact Hlookup.
  - (* T_Abs *)
    simpl. split.
    + apply SN_abs.
    + intros v Hvval HvR.
      apply R_abs_app with (body := subst_fvar sigma t1).
      intros w HwR.
      pose (x0 := fresh (L ++ fv t1)).
      assert (Hx0L : ~ In x0 L).
      { intro Hin. apply (fresh_not_in (L ++ fv t1)). apply in_app_iff. left. exact Hin. }
      assert (Hx0fv : ~ In x0 (fv t1)).
      { intro Hin. apply (fresh_not_in (L ++ fv t1)). apply in_app_iff. right. exact Hin. }
      pose (sigma' := fun y => if Nat.eqb x0 y then w else sigma y).
      assert (Hx0w : sigma' x0 = w).
      { unfold sigma'. rewrite Nat.eqb_refl. reflexivity. }
      assert (Hgood' : good (update Gamma0 x0 T1) sigma').
      { intros y U Hy. unfold sigma'.
        destruct (Nat.eqb x0 y) eqn:E.
        - apply Nat.eqb_eq in E. subst y.
          rewrite update_eq in Hy. injection Hy as HU. subst U.
          rewrite Nat.eqb_refl. exact HwR.
        - unfold update in Hy. rewrite E in Hy.
          apply Hgood. exact Hy. }
      specialize (IHbody x0 Hx0L sigma' Hgood').
      rewrite subst_fvar_open in IHbody.
      simpl in IHbody.
      rewrite Hx0w in IHbody.
      assert (Hagree : subst_fvar sigma' t1 = subst_fvar sigma t1).
      { apply subst_fvar_agree. intros x Hx. unfold sigma'.
        destruct (Nat.eqb x0 x) eqn:E.
        - apply Nat.eqb_eq in E. subst x. contradiction.
        - reflexivity. }
      rewrite Hagree in IHbody.
      exact IHbody.
  - (* T_App *)
    simpl. apply R_app with (A := T1) (B := T2).
    + apply IH1. exact Hgood.
    + apply IH2. exact Hgood.
  - (* T_True *) simpl. apply SN_true.
  - (* T_False *) simpl. apply SN_false.
Qed.
```

Let me draft the auxiliary block text.

```coq
(** ** Strong normalization proof.

    We use a unary, type-indexed logical relation over terms.  The relation
    is defined on all terms (free and bound variables included); the typing
    context is interpreted by a substitution mapping atoms to terms. *)

(** Substitution of free (named) variables.  Since binders are locally
    nameless, this is a plain homomorphism: no shifting is required. *)

Fixpoint subst_fvar (sigma : atom -> tm) (t : tm) : tm :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar i => tm_bvar i
  | tm_fvar x => sigma x
  | tm_app t1 t2 => tm_app (subst_fvar sigma t1) (subst_fvar sigma t2)
  | tm_abs T t1 => tm_abs T (subst_fvar sigma t1)
  | tm_true => tm_true
  | tm_false => tm_false
  end.

(** Free atoms of a term. *)

Fixpoint fv (t : tm) : list atom :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar _ => nil
  | tm_fvar x => x :: nil
  | tm_app t1 t2 => fv t1 ++ fv t2
  | tm_abs _ t1 => fv t1
  | tm_true => nil
  | tm_false => nil
  end.

(** An atom fresh for a finite list. *)

Fixpoint atom_max (L : list atom) : atom :=
  match L with
  | nil => 0
  | x :: L' => Nat.max x (atom_max L')
  end.

Definition fresh (L : list atom) : atom := S (atom_max L).

Lemma in_atom_max : forall L x, In x L -> x <= atom_max L.
Proof.
  induction L as [| y L IH]; intros x Hx; simpl in *.
  - contradiction.
  - destruct Hx as [-> | Hx].
    + apply Nat.le_max_l.
    + apply Nat.le_trans with (atom_max L); [apply IH; exact Hx | apply Nat.le_max_r].
Qed.

Lemma fresh_not_in : forall L, ~ In (fresh L) L.
Proof.
  intros L Hin. apply in_atom_max in Hin. unfold fresh in Hin. lia.
Qed.

(** Opening and free-variable substitution commute. *)

Lemma subst_fvar_open_rec : forall sigma t k u,
  subst_fvar sigma (open_rec k u t) =
  open_rec k (subst_fvar sigma u) (subst_fvar sigma t).
Proof.
  intros sigma t. induction t as [i|x|t1 IH1 t2 IH2|T t IH| |]; intros k u; simpl.
  - destruct (Nat.eqb k i); reflexivity.
  - reflexivity.
  - rewrite IH1, IH2; reflexivity.
  - rewrite IH with (k := S k); reflexivity.
  - reflexivity.
  - reflexivity.
Qed.

Lemma subst_fvar_open : forall sigma t u,
  subst_fvar sigma (open t u) = open (subst_fvar sigma t) (subst_fvar sigma u).
Proof. intros. unfold open. apply subst_fvar_open_rec. Qed.

Lemma subst_fvar_id : forall t, subst_fvar (fun x => tm_fvar x) t = t.
Proof.
  induction t as [i|x|t1 IH1 t2 IH2|T t IH| |]; simpl.
  - reflexivity.
  - reflexivity.
  - rewrite IH1, IH2; reflexivity.
  - rewrite IH; reflexivity.
  - reflexivity.
  - reflexivity.
Qed.

Lemma subst_fvar_agree : forall sigma1 sigma2 t,
  (forall x, In x (fv t) -> sigma1 x = sigma2 x) ->
  subst_fvar sigma1 t = subst_fvar sigma2 t.
Proof.
  intros sigma1 sigma2 t. induction t as [i|x|t1 IH1 t2 IH2|T t IH| |]; intros Hagree; simpl.
  - reflexivity.
  - apply Hagree. simpl. auto.
  - f_equal.
    + apply IH1. intros x Hx. apply Hagree. apply in_app_iff. left. exact Hx.
    + apply IH2. intros x Hx. apply Hagree. apply in_app_iff. right. exact Hx.
  - f_equal. apply IH. intros x Hx. apply Hagree. exact Hx.
  - reflexivity.
  - reflexivity.
Qed.
```

Then:
```coq
(** Basic facts about values, steps and strong normalization. *)

Lemma value_lc : forall v, value v -> locally_closed v.
Proof.
  intros v Hv. induction Hv as [T t Hlc | | ].
  - exact Hlc.
  - apply lc_true.
  - apply lc_false.
Qed.

Lemma abs_no_step : forall T t t', ~ (tm_abs T t --> t').
Proof. intros T t t' H; inversion H. Qed.

Lemma value_no_step : forall v, value v -> forall t', ~ (v --> t').
Proof.
  intros v Hv. induction Hv as [T t Hlc | | ]; intros t' H.
  - apply (abs_no_step T t t'); exact H.
  - inversion H.
  - inversion H.
Qed.

Lemma app_not_value : forall t1 t2, ~ value (tm_app t1 t2).
Proof. intros t1 t2 H; inversion H. Qed.

Lemma SN_step : forall t t', strongly_normalizing t -> t --> t' -> strongly_normalizing t'.
Proof.
  intros t t' HSN Hstep. inversion HSN as [u Hred]. apply Hred. exact Hstep.
Qed.

Lemma SN_abs : forall T t, strongly_normalizing (tm_abs T t).
Proof.
  intros T t. apply SN_intro. intros t' H. exfalso. apply (abs_no_step T t t'). exact H.
Qed.

Lemma SN_true : strongly_normalizing tm_true.
Proof. apply SN_intro. intros t' H. inversion H. Qed.

Lemma SN_false : strongly_normalizing tm_false.
Proof. apply SN_intro. intros t' H. inversion H. Qed.

(** Deciding whether a term is a value. *)

Lemma lc_at_dec : forall t k, lc_at k t \/ ~ lc_at k t.
... (as tested)

Lemma value_abs_lc : forall T t, value (tm_abs T t) -> lc_at 1 t.
Proof.
  intros T t H. inversion H as [T' t' Hlc | | ]. inversion Hlc. assumption.
Qed.

Lemma value_dec : forall t, value t \/ ~ value t.
... (as tested)

(** The logical relation. *)

Fixpoint R (T : ty) (t : tm) : Prop := ...
```
